A generator of each line can change by a unit; this changes neither vanishing nor nonvanishing of any finite-level order. We will use this observation only after proving that the residue characteristics differ from $\ell$.

<a id="local:section"></a>
### Recovering the local data

The commutator detects a relation between multiplicative characters. That relation recovers quasi-prime divisors, including those that value constants nontrivially. At this stage their residue fields may have characteristic $\ell$. We therefore work with character groups throughout and use a Galois interpretation only at the top field. For the commuting-pair approach to valuation detection and its development via rigid elements, see (F. A. Bogomolov 1991; F. Bogomolov and Tschinkel 2002; Topaz 2017) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Bogomolov1991](#ref-Bogomolov1991), [BT2002](#ref-BT2002), [TopazCL2017](#ref-TopazCL2017)]. We use the character-space formulation below to include the required residue fields.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Commutators and alternating characters

<a id="local:alternating"></a>
For any field $F$, put $$\mathcal G_F=W_F\otimes_{\mathbb Z_\ell}\mathbb Q_\ell.$$ A pair $f,g\in\mathcal G_F$ is *alternating* if $$\label{local:alternating}
 f(x)g(1-x)=f(1-x)g(x)\qquad(x\in F\setminus\{0,1\}).$$ A subspace is alternating if each pair of its elements is alternating. Every character in $\mathcal G_F$ kills $-1$.

<a id="stmt-local-commuting"></a>
<div id="local:commuting" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6**. *Let $F/\kappa$ be a function field over an algebraically closed field of characteristic different from $\ell$. Under Kummer duality $W_F=\Pi_F^a$, two elements of $W_F$ are alternating if and only if their commutator in $\Delta_F$ is zero. This assertion holds also for $\ell=2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-commuting"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-commuting</summary>

*Proof.* We give the finite-coefficient argument, keeping track of the infinite rank of $W_F$. Write $G=G_F^{(\ell)}$, $A=G^{\mathrm{ab}}$ and $R=\mathbb Z/\ell^e$, with trivial action. Kummer duality makes $A$ a product of copies of $\mathbb Z_\ell$.

First, a class in $H^2_{\mathrm{cont}}(A,R)$ is determined by its commutator form. Indeed, a class with zero commutator defines an abelian extension of $A$ by $R$. Such an extension splits continuously: the Pontryagin dual of $A$ is a direct sum of copies of $\mathbb Q_\ell/\mathbb Z_\ell$, hence is divisible and injective. Conversely every continuous alternating bilinear form $A\times A\to R$ is the commutator form of a cup-product class. To see this, continuity and compactness give an open subgroup in the radical of the form, so it depends on finitely many coordinates. In coordinate characters $x_i$ it is a sum of the forms $$x_i(a)x_j(b)-x_j(a)x_i(b),\qquad i<j,$$ which come from the cocycles $x_i\cup x_j$. Consequently cup products generate $H^2_{\mathrm{cont}}(A,R)$. This proof uses no division by $2$. In particular, even when $\ell=2$, a diagonal cup has zero commutator and therefore vanishes. No extra power or Bockstein class survives on the torsion-free group $A$.

Next, inflation $$H^2_{\mathrm{cont}}(G,R)\longrightarrow H^2(G_F,R)$$ is injective. Let $F(\ell)$ be the maximal Galois pro-$\ell$ extension of $F$. An element of $F(\ell)^\times$ lies in a finite Galois $\ell$-extension $F'/F$. Adjoin to $F'$ the $\ell^e$-th roots of all its $F$-conjugates. Since the roots of unity lie in $F$, the resulting field is again a finite Galois $\ell$-extension of $F$. Thus $F(\ell)^\times$ is $\ell^e$-divisible. Kummer theory gives $H^1(G_{F(\ell)},R)=0$, and the five-term sequence proves the claimed injectivity.

Fix compatible identifications of the roots of unity with the coefficient groups. The degree-two norm-residue theorem (Merkurjev and Suslin 1983) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[MS1982](#ref-MS1982)] identifies the kernel of the tensor cup map $$(F^\times/F^{\times\ell^e})\otimes_R
 (F^\times/F^{\times\ell^e})\longrightarrow H^2(G_F,R)$$ with the subgroup generated by the Steinberg tensors $[x]\otimes[1-x]$. Since the tensor cup map onto $H^2_{\mathrm{cont}}(A,R)$ is surjective, the kernel of inflation from $A$ to $G$ is generated by the Steinberg cups $[x]\cup[1-x]$.

Finally, put $N=[G,G]$. The five-term sequence for $N\subset G$ identifies this same kernel with $$\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{cont}}(N/[N,G],R)
 =\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{cont}}(\Delta_F,R).$$ The commutator form of the transgression of a character $\chi$ is $\chi([\ ,\ ]_F)$, up to the uniform sign determined by the transgression convention. Its value at $f,g$ therefore vanishes for all $\chi$ precisely when $$f(x)g(1-x)-f(1-x)g(x)=0\pmod{\ell^e}$$ for every $x\ne0,1$. Continuous characters to finite cyclic $\ell$-groups separate points of $\Delta_F$. Letting $e$ vary proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

It follows that a bracket-compatible isomorphism preserves alternating pairs on $W_F$, and hence on $\mathcal G_F$: multiply rationalized characters by powers of $\ell$ and use <a href="#local:alternating" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:alternating">[local:alternating]</a>. The latter relation makes sense on residue fields of every characteristic, without invoking their Galois groups.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Character groups of valuations

We regard equivalent valuations as the same valuation. Write $v\leq w$ if $v$ is a coarsening of $w$. For a valuation $v$ of $F$, let $\mathcal O_v$, $\mathfrak m_v$, $Fv$ and $vF$ denote its valuation ring, maximal ideal, residue field and value group. Set $$U_v=\mathcal O_v^\times,\qquad U_v^1=1+\mathfrak m_v,
 \qquad I_v=U_v^\perp\subset D_v=(U_v^1)^\perp\subset W_F,$$ and write $\mathcal I_v=I_v\otimes\mathbb Q_\ell$ and $\mathcal D_v=D_v\otimes\mathbb Q_\ell$. These are the *minimized* inertia and decomposition groups. When both $F$ and $Fv$ have characteristic different from $\ell$, they agree with the usual abelian inertia and decomposition groups. Their definitions above impose no characteristic restriction.

A subset of $\mathcal G_F$ is *valuative* if it is contained in $\mathcal I_v$ for some valuation $v$. We use the following local character theorem (Topaz 2019, Fact 2.1, Lemma 2.5 and Corollary 2.7) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Topaz2019](#ref-Topaz2019)]:

1.  Every valuative subset $\Sigma$ has a unique coarsest valuation $v_\Sigma$ with $\Sigma\subset\mathcal I_{v_\Sigma}$. It coarsens every valuation with this property.

2.  In an alternating subspace $S$, the valuative elements form a valuative subspace $\Sigma$ of codimension at most one, and $S\subset\mathcal D_{v_\Sigma}$.

3.  If $\Sigma$ is valuative and a character $f$ alternates with every member of $\Sigma$, then $f\in\mathcal D_{v_\Sigma}$.

These statements use $\operatorname{Hom}(F^\times,\mathbb Z_\ell)\otimes\mathbb Q_\ell$ and hold for arbitrary fields $F$. The condition on the value at $-1$ in the local character theorem is automatic here. This is the form needed below when $\operatorname{char}(Fv)=\ell$.

<a id="local:rank"></a>
Let now $F/\kappa$ be a function field over an algebraically closed field, of transcendence degree $s$. A *quasi-prime divisor* is a valuation minimal among those satisfying $$vF/v\kappa\cong\mathbb Z,
 \qquad \operatorname{trdeg}(Fv/\kappa v)=s-1.$$ A quasi-prime $r$-divisor is a composition of $r$ successive quasi-prime divisors of the successive residue function fields. It satisfies $$\label{local:rank}
 vF/v\kappa\cong\mathbb Z^r,
 \qquad \operatorname{trdeg}(Fv/\kappa v)=s-r.$$ The residue constants $\kappa v$ are algebraically closed.

<a id="local:abhyankar"></a>
We recall the finite-generation point implicit in <a href="#local:rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:rank">[local:rank]</a>. The valuation transcendence-degree inequality is $$\label{local:abhyankar}
 \operatorname{rrank}(vF/v\kappa)
 +\operatorname{trdeg}(Fv/\kappa v)\leq s,$$ where $\operatorname{rrank}$ is dimension after tensoring with $\mathbb Q$. At equality, choose relative value-independent elements and elements with algebraically independent residues. Together they form a transcendence basis, and $F$ is finite over the resulting rational field. The finite-extension valuation inequality then shows that the relative value group is finitely generated and the residue extension is finitely generated. Since $v\kappa$ is divisible, $vF/v\kappa$ is torsion free, hence is a finitely generated free abelian group. Applying this argument successively gives <a href="#local:rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:rank">[local:rank]</a>.

<a id="stmt-local-residue"></a>
<div id="local:residue" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7**. *For a quasi-prime $r$-divisor $v$ of $F/\kappa$, restriction to units induces a canonical topological isomorphism $$D_v/I_v\cong W_{Fv}.$$ If $f,g\in\mathcal D_v$, they are alternating if and only if their residual characters in $\mathcal G_{Fv}$ are alternating.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-residue"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-residue</summary>

*Proof.* There is an exact sequence of abelian groups $$0\longrightarrow (Fv)^\times/(\kappa v)^\times
 \longrightarrow F^\times/(\kappa^\times U_v^1)
 \longrightarrow vF/v\kappa\longrightarrow0.$$ Its final term is free, so applying $\operatorname{Hom}(-,\mathbb Z_\ell)$ gives a surjection $D_v\to W_{Fv}$ with kernel $I_v$. This map is continuous for pointwise convergence; compactness makes the induced bijection on the quotient a topological isomorphism.

For the second assertion, use <a href="#local:alternating" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:alternating">[local:alternating]</a>. If $v(x)>0$, then $1-x$ is a principal unit. If $v(x)<0$, the identity $1-x=-x(1-x^{-1})$ makes the characters of $1-x$ equal those of $x$. If $v(x)=0$ and $v(1-x)>0$, then $x$ is a principal unit. In each case the alternating determinant is zero. In the remaining case $x$ and $1-x$ are units with residues different from $0,1$, and the determinant is exactly the residual determinant. Conversely every residual element different from $0,1$ has such a lift. ◻

</details>

In particular, a unit representing a class modulo $\ell^e$ evaluates on $D_v/I_v$ exactly as its residue does on $W_{Fv}$. This observation will allow finite Kummer tests to pass through a valuation.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### An intrinsic description of quasi-prime divisors

The next argument follows the character-space proof of (Topaz 2019, Fact 3.2 and Theorem 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Topaz2019](#ref-Topaz2019)]. We include it because we need it also in residue characteristic $\ell$, whereas that section of the cited paper is stated in a cohomological setting with characteristic different from $\ell$.

<a id="stmt-local-dimension"></a>
<div id="local:dimension" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8**. *For $F/\kappa$ as above, in any characteristic, $s$ is the maximum dimension of an alternating subspace of $\mathcal G_F$. If $S$ has dimension $s$, its valuative subspace is $\mathcal I_u$ for its associated valuation $u$, and $u$ attains equality in <a href="#local:abhyankar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:abhyankar">[local:abhyankar]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-dimension"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-dimension</summary>

*Proof.* For every valuation $u$, characters kill the divisible constant group, so $$\dim_{\mathbb Q_\ell}\mathcal I_u
 \leq\operatorname{rrank}(uF/u\kappa).$$ Let $S$ be alternating and let $\Sigma$ be its valuative subspace, with associated valuation $u$. If $S=\Sigma$, the asserted dimension bound follows from <a href="#local:abhyankar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:abhyankar">[local:abhyankar]</a>. Otherwise $\Sigma$ has codimension one in $S$. A character in $S\setminus\Sigma\subset\mathcal D_u$ has nonzero residual character. Thus $Fu\ne\kappa u$, and $\operatorname{trdeg}(Fu/\kappa u)\geq1$. Consequently $$\dim S\leq\operatorname{rrank}(uF/u\kappa)+1\leq s.$$ This also bounds arbitrary alternating subspaces, by applying the argument to their finite-dimensional subspaces.

There is a full discrete flag at a smooth closed point on a model of $F/\kappa$. Its inertia space has dimension $s$ and is alternating by the valuation inequality for $x$ and $1-x$. Thus the maximum equals $s$.

If $\dim S=s$ and $S=\Sigma$, all inequalities $$s\leq\dim\mathcal I_u
 \leq\operatorname{rrank}(uF/u\kappa)\leq s$$ are equalities. If $S\ne\Sigma$, all inequalities $$s-1\leq\dim\mathcal I_u
 \leq\operatorname{rrank}(uF/u\kappa)
 \leq s-\operatorname{trdeg}(Fu/\kappa u)\leq s-1$$ are equalities. In both cases $\Sigma=\mathcal I_u$ and $u$ has no transcendence defect. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-local-quasiprime"></a>
<div id="local:quasiprime" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9**. *Assume $s\geq2$. A line $H\subset\mathcal G_F$ is the inertia space of a quasi-prime divisor if and only if there are two $s$-dimensional alternating subspaces $S_1,S_2$ with $S_1\cap S_2=H$. The valuation is unique. If $0\ne h\in H$, its decomposition space is $$\mathcal D_v=\{f\in\mathcal G_F:(h,f)\text{ is alternating}\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-quasiprime"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-quasiprime</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose first that $v$ is quasi-prime. The residue function field $Fv/\kappa v$ has two independent discrete valuations of full rank $s-1$, trivial on the constants. For example, choose two independent prime divisors on a rational subfield on a transcendence basis, complete each to a flag, and prolong the flags to the finite extension $Fv$. Independent valuation approximation (Anscombe, Dittmann, and Fehm 2020, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[ADF2020](#ref-ADF2020)] makes their inertia spaces disjoint. Composing with $v$ gives two alternating inertia spaces of dimension $s$ whose intersection is $\mathcal I_v$.

Conversely, let $S_1,S_2$ be as stated. By Lemma <a href="#local:dimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:dimension">8</a>, their valuative parts are $\Sigma_i=\mathcal I_{u_i}$, where each $u_i$ has no transcendence defect and $\dim\Sigma_i\geq s-1$. We first show that $H$ is valuative. If, say, $u_1\leq u_2$, then $$\Sigma_1\subset\Sigma_2,
 \qquad \Sigma_1\subset S_1\cap S_2=H.$$ Since $\dim\Sigma_1\geq1$, this proves the claim. If the valuations are incomparable, let $w$ be their finest common coarsening. Applying independent valuation approximation on $Fw$ shows that the principal-unit groups of $u_1$ and $u_2$ generate $U_w$. Indeed, approximate a unit $a$ by $b$ for the first residual valuation and approximate $1$ by $b$ for the second. Then $a=(a/b)b$ is a product of the two required principal units. A character in $H$ annihilates both principal-unit groups, and hence belongs to $\mathcal I_w$. Again $H$ is valuative. In either case, $$H=\Sigma_1\cap\Sigma_2.$$

Let $v=v_H$ be its coarsest associated valuation. It coarsens both $u_i$, and $$H\subset\mathcal I_v
 \subset\mathcal I_{u_1}\cap\mathcal I_{u_2}=H.$$ A coarsening of a valuation without transcendence defect also has no transcendence defect: relative rational ranks add under composition, and the two valuation inequalities sum to the equality for the finer valuation. Thus $v$ has no transcendence defect. Its relative value group is finitely generated free, and its rank is $\dim\mathcal I_v=1$. Hence $vF/v\kappa\cong\mathbb Z$. A coarsening with the same relative rank would have the same inertia space, contradicting the defining minimality of $v_H$. Therefore $v$ is quasi-prime, and the coarsest-valuation description also proves uniqueness.

Every member of $\mathcal D_v$ alternates with $h$, by the calculation in Lemma <a href="#local:residue" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:residue">7</a>. For the converse apply the third part of the local character theorem to the valuative line $H$. ◻

</details>

The integral groups are recovered without an index ambiguity: $$I_v=\mathcal I_v\cap W_F,
 \qquad D_v=\mathcal D_v\cap W_F.$$ Indeed these are annihilators with values in the torsion-free group $\mathbb Z_\ell$. In particular they are saturated submodules.

<a id="stmt-local-transport"></a>
<div id="local:transport" class="proposition">

**Proposition 10**. *Let $K/k$ and $L/l$ be function fields over algebraically closed fields of characteristic different from $\ell$, each of transcendence degree at least two. Let $\varphi:W_L\to W_K$ be a continuous bracket-compatible $\mathbb Z_\ell$-module isomorphism. Then their transcendence degrees agree, say they are $d$. For every $1\leq r<d$, the map $\varphi$ carries exactly the pairs $I_w\subset D_w$ of quasi-prime $r$-divisors of $L/l$ onto the corresponding pairs of $K/k$. For matched pairs it induces a continuous isomorphism $$D_w/I_w\cong W_{Lw}\ \longrightarrow\
 D_v/I_v\cong W_{Kv}$$ that preserves alternating pairs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-transport"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-transport</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#local:commuting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:commuting">6</a> makes the alternating relation intrinsic to the bracket. Lemma <a href="#local:dimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:dimension">8</a> then recovers $d$, and Lemma <a href="#local:quasiprime" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:quasiprime">9</a> recovers the rationalized rank-one pairs. Intersecting with the integral modules recovers the exact pairs. Lemma <a href="#local:residue" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:residue">7</a> supplies the residual character spaces and their alternating relation. If $u$ is a valuation of $Fv$, the inverse images of $I_u$ and $D_u$ under $D_v\to W_{Fv}$ are $I_{u\circ v}$ and $D_{u\circ v}$, respectively, directly from the unit and principal-unit definitions. Apply the same characteristic-free recognition argument to each residue function field. Continuing while its transcendence degree is at least two recovers precisely the successive quasi-prime divisors through rank $d-1$. The construction applies to $\varphi^{-1}$ as well. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Recognizing the finite-constant residual curves

The remaining local input recognizes certain residual curves and all their point inertia groups. It does not yet distinguish true divisors from quasi-prime divisors on the original function field.

We first describe the topological test. A compact abelian pro-$\ell$ group $G$ with a family $(T_i)$ of procyclic subgroups is *complete-curve-like* if one can choose generators $\tau_i$ so that $\tau_i$ tends to zero outside finite subsets, the resulting map $$\prod_i\mathbb Z_\ell\longrightarrow G,
 \qquad(a_i)\longmapsto\sum_i a_i\tau_i$$ has kernel the diagonal copy of $\mathbb Z_\ell$, and its cokernel is a finitely generated $\mathbb Z_\ell$-module. The diagonal kernel expresses the single relation among the point inertias of a complete curve. This property is invariant under continuous module isomorphisms.

<a id="local:residual-set"></a>
For this criterion, let $F/\kappa$ be a function field over an algebraically closed field of characteristic different from $\ell$; its residue fields may still have characteristic $\ell$. Form the closed subset $$\mathscr I_F=
 \overline{\bigcup_{u\text{ quasi-prime}} I_u}\subset W_F.$$ This is the closure of a *union*, not the closed subgroup generated by that union. For a quasi-prime $(s-1)$-divisor $v$, let $$\label{local:residual-set}
 \mathscr R_v=
 \operatorname{im}\bigl(\mathscr I_F\cap D_v\longrightarrow
 D_v/I_v\bigr),$$ and consider all maximal procyclic subgroups of $D_v/I_v$ contained in $\mathscr R_v$.

Pop’s residual-curve criterion (Pop 2015, sec. 4, Theorem 4.2(i)(a)) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[PopMin2015](#ref-PopMin2015)] applies when

1.  $vF$ has no nonzero $\ell$-divisible convex subgroup;

2.  there is a subfield $\kappa_1\subset F$ with $\kappa_1v=\kappa v$ and $\operatorname{trdeg}(F/\kappa_1)
     =\operatorname{trdeg}(Fv/\kappa_1v)=1$.

It states that the group with the family just described is complete-curve-like exactly when $\kappa v$ is an algebraic closure of a finite field. In that case the family is precisely all point inertia subgroups of the smooth projective curve with function field $Fv$. The criterion allows $\operatorname{char}(Fv)=\ell$; this is the purpose of the minimized groups and of Topaz’s Appendix to (Pop 2015) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[PopMin2015](#ref-PopMin2015)].

<a id="stmt-local-criterion-hypotheses"></a>
<div id="local:criterion-hypotheses" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11**. *Every quasi-prime $(s-1)$-divisor $v$ of $F/\kappa$ satisfies the two hypotheses of the residual-curve criterion.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-criterion-hypotheses"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-criterion-hypotheses</summary>

*Proof.* For a quasi-prime divisor $u$, an $\ell$-divisible convex subgroup $H\subset uF$ has zero image in $uF/u\kappa\cong\mathbb Z$. Thus $H\subset u\kappa$. Coarsening by $H$ preserves the relative rank one. Since $u$ attains equality in the valuation inequality, its coarsening does also, so its residual transcendence degree is still $s-1$. Minimality forces $H=0$.

Now consider a composite quasi-prime divisor. The value group of its last residual step is a nonzero convex subgroup $\Gamma$ of the full value group and has the rank-one property just proved. If $H$ were a nonzero $\ell$-divisible convex subgroup of the full group, convexity would make $H\cap\Gamma$ $\ell$-divisible: an $\ell$-th part of a positive element remains between zero and that element. Convex subgroups are comparable, so $H\cap\Gamma$ is nonzero. This contradicts the rank-one case.

For the second hypothesis choose $x_1,\ldots,x_{s-1}$ whose values are rationally independent modulo $v\kappa$, and put $\kappa_1=\kappa(x_1,\ldots,x_{s-1})$. Relative value independence implies algebraic independence. In a polynomial in the $x_i$, distinct monomials have different values modulo $v\kappa$, and there is a unique term of least value. A quotient of polynomials with value zero consequently has residue in $\kappa v$: equality of the two least values forces the same monomial, leaving a quotient of constants. It follows that $\kappa_1v=\kappa v$. The two asserted transcendence degrees are then one by <a href="#local:rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:rank">[local:rank]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-local-curves"></a>
<div id="local:curves" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12**. *In the setting of Proposition <a href="#local:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:transport">10</a>, let $w$ and $v$ be matched quasi-prime $(d-1)$-divisors. Then $lw$ is algebraic over a finite field if and only if $kv$ is. When this holds, the induced isomorphism $W_{Lw}\to W_{Kv}$ carries the full family of point inertia subgroups of the respective smooth projective curves onto each other. Corresponding normalized order characters differ by units of $\mathbb Z_\ell$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-local-curves"><summary>Proof of stmt-local-curves</summary>

*Proof.* Proposition <a href="#local:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:transport">10</a> recovers every group used in <a href="#local:residual-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="local:residual-set">[local:residual-set]</a>. A continuous isomorphism preserves the closure of the union, its intersection with decomposition, its quotient image and the maximal procyclic subgroups in that image. Lemma <a href="#local:criterion-hypotheses" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:criterion-hypotheses">11</a> verifies the hypotheses of Pop’s criterion on both sides. The complete-curve-like property therefore holds simultaneously, and the criterion identifies the entire point families when it does. The resulting isomorphism identifies each full procyclic subgroup with its counterpart, so it sends a generator to a generator, differing only by a $\mathbb Z_\ell$-unit. ◻

</details>

Only after separately excluding residue characteristic $\ell$ will we use these point lines to compare genera. For the present purpose, Proposition <a href="#local:curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:curves">12</a> provides exact point-order vanishing at every finite level, with no assumption on the cardinality or transcendence degree of the original constant fields.

<a id="sec:residual-tests"></a>
### Finite tests on residual curves

<span id="sec:tests" label="sec:tests"></span>

We next construct residual curves that retain any prescribed finite collection of Kummer relations. The curve can depend on the functions and on the exponent. Its genus, however, is bounded in terms of one fixed projective model. This distinction will make the pencil estimate uniform.

Fix a normal integral projective model $X\subset\mathbb P^N_l$ of $L/l$, and put $d=\operatorname{trdeg}(L/l)\ge2$. For a prime divisor $D$ on $X$, let $\deg D$ denote its degree in this embedding. For an integer $m\ge2$ and $g\in L^\times$, set $$s_m(g)=\sum_{\substack{D\subset X\text{ prime divisor}\\
                       \operatorname{ord}_D(g)\not\equiv0\pmod m}}
                 \deg D.$$ This is a finite sum.

<a id="stmt-tests-residual"></a>
<div id="tests:residual" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Finite residual tests). *There is an integer $G_X\ge0$, depending only on the embedded variety $X$, with the following property. Given $g_1,\ldots,g_r\in L^\times$ and $q=\ell^e$, $e\ge1$, there is a quasi-prime $(d-1)$-divisor $w$ of $L/l$ such that:*

1.  *$lw$ is an algebraic closure of a finite field of characteristic different from $\ell$, and $Lw=lw(C)$ for a smooth projective integral curve $C/lw$ with $g(C)\le G_X$;*

2.  *$w(g_j)=0$ for every $j$, and, for every $(n_1,\ldots,n_r)\in(\mathbb Z/q)^r$, $$\prod_jg_j^{n_j}\in L^{\times q}
     \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
     \prod_j\overline g_j^{n_j}\in(Lw)^{\times q};$$*

<a id="tests:support"></a>
3.  *for $m=\ell$ and $m=q$, $$\label{tests:support}
     \#\{P\in C(lw):\operatorname{ord}_P(\overline g_j)
                                      \not\equiv0\pmod m\}=s_m(g_j)
     \qquad(1\le j\le r).$$*

*Here $\overline g_j$ is the residue of $g_j$ at $w$.*

</div>

We use the following form of Bertini irreducibility. Its hypotheses include the finite covering maps that occur below.

<a id="stmt-tests-bertini"></a>
<div id="tests:bertini" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Hyperplane sections of a quasi-finite map). *Let $V$ be an integral variety over an algebraically closed field, and let $f:V\to\mathbb P^N$ be quasi-finite, with $\dim V=a\ge2$. For a general hyperplane $H$, the inverse image $f^{-1}(H)$ is geometrically irreducible. If $f$ factors as a finite étale map to a smooth locally closed subvariety $U\subset\mathbb P^N$, this inverse image is also smooth for a general $H$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tests-bertini"><summary>Proof of stmt-tests-bertini</summary>

*Proof.* We recall the incidence argument for irreducibility; see also (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Lemma 37.32.3, Tag 0G4F) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. Write $B=(\mathbb P^N)^*$ and $$I=\{(x,H)\in V\times B:f(x)\in H\}.$$ The projection to $V$ is a projective bundle, so $I$ is integral. In $I\times_BI$, the open set lying over pairs $(x,y)$ with $f(x)\ne f(y)$ is a projective bundle with fiber $\mathbb P^{N-2}$ over an irreducible open subset of $V\times V$. It is therefore irreducible of dimension $2a+N-2$. The locus of pairs with $f(x)=f(y)$ has dimension at most $a$, since $f$ is quasi-finite; the incidence above it has dimension at most $a+N-1$. This is strictly smaller than $2a+N-2$.

Every component of the generic hyperplane section has dimension $a-1$: a hyperplane cuts a nonzero nonunit in an integral variety of finite type over a field. Consequently every component of its square has dimension $2a-2$. The equal-image locus cannot supply a component of this generic square. The generic square is thus irreducible. Equivalently, the function field of $B$ is separably algebraically closed in that of $I$, so the generic hyperplane section is geometrically irreducible. Geometric irreducibility spreads to a nonempty open of $B$ (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Lemma 37.27.5, Tag 0559) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. Finally, a general hyperplane section of the smooth embedded variety $U$ is smooth by Bertini, and its étale inverse image is smooth. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-tests-bertini-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-tests-bertini</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#tests:residual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tests:residual">13</a>.* *Spread the finite data.* Let $D_1,\ldots,D_s$ be the prime divisors occurring in the divisors of the $g_j$. There is a closed subset $B_X\subset X$ of codimension at least two such that $X\setminus B_X$ is smooth, the $D_i\setminus B_X$ are smooth pairwise disjoint Cartier divisors, and, near each of them, every $g_j$ is a power of a local equation times a unit. Off their union the $g_j$ are units. These assertions follow by deleting the singular loci, the pairwise divisor intersections and the proper closed exceptional subsets on which the required local identities fail.

Choose a smooth dense open $U\subset X$ on which all $g_j$ are units. The simultaneous root cover $$V=\operatorname{Spec}_U
 \mathcal O_U[T_1,\ldots,T_r]/(T_1^q-g_1,\ldots,T_r^q-g_r)
 \longrightarrow U$$ is finite étale of degree $q^r$. Let $H$ be the subgroup generated by the $[g_j]$ in $L^\times/L^{\times q}$. Kummer theory shows that each connected component of $V$ has degree $|H|$ over $U$. Since $U$ is smooth, these components are integral.

All these data descend to a finitely generated domain $R\subset l$ in which $\ell$ is invertible. Enlarge $R$ and then localize it so that the following properties hold in every geometric fiber:

- the embedded model $X_R$ is projective and flat, with normal integral fibers and the Hilbert polynomial of $X$;

- the $D_{i,R}$ are distinct geometrically integral divisors with their original degrees, and the local divisor identities and smoothness conditions above hold outside a subset of fiberwise codimension at least two;

- the root cover and its disjoint decomposition into components extend, and every component has geometrically integral fibers and finite locally free degree $|H|$ over $U_R$.

These are finite spreading conditions. Flatness makes the projective Hilbert polynomials constant. Geometric integrality and normality spread from the generic fiber after shrinking; the latter uses the proper flat finite-presentation form of (Grothendieck 1966, Theorem 12.2.4(iv),(viii)) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[EGAIV3](#ref-EGAIV3)]. The equations $g_j=u\pi^n$, the inverses specifying units, the component idempotents, and the finite locally free ranks are all finite algebraic data. Spreading them preserves the exact orders and degrees, rather than just the supports. The dimension bound on the omitted closed set also persists after shrinking.

For later use include a separating transcendence basis $x_1,\ldots,x_d$ of $L/l$, a primitive element $y$ over $l(x_1,\ldots,x_d)$, its monic minimal polynomial, and common birational opens with $X$. After another localization, the same presentation and birational identification hold in the geometric fibers, with the reduced polynomial irreducible of its original degree over the rational function field. Preserve as well the nonzero reductions of the finitely many numerators and denominators used for the $g_j$. This follows either by spreading those common opens and geometric integrality, or by spreading the absolutely irreducible defining polynomial after clearing denominators.

*Realize a good fiber as a residue field.* Choose a closed point $s\in\operatorname{Spec}R$. Its residue field is finite and has characteristic different from $\ell$. A valuation ring of $l$ dominating $R_s$ exists (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Lemma 10.50.2, Tag 00IA) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. Its residue field is algebraically closed. If that residue field is not algebraic over a finite field, give a transcendence basis independent values in an ordered free abelian group, prolong the resulting valuation to the residue field, and compose. This yields a valuation $s_0$ of $l$ with the same center $s$ and residue field $\lambda$ algebraic over a finite field. Residues of algebraically closed fields are algebraically closed, so $\lambda$ is an algebraic closure of a finite field. The composition does not change the center because every nonzero element of a finite field has value zero. This construction uses a transcendence basis of arbitrary cardinality; there is no restriction on the size of $l$.

Extend $s_0$ to $l(x_1,\ldots,x_d)$ by the Gauss valuation, whose residue field is $\lambda(x_1,\ldots,x_d)$ and whose value group is $s_0l$. Prolong it to $L$. The element $y$ is integral and its residue satisfies the reduced irreducible polynomial, of degree $[L:l(x_1,\ldots,x_d)]$. The fundamental valuation inequality therefore forces the residue degree to be that degree and the value-group index to be one (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, sec. 15.125, Tag 0ASF) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. We obtain a valuation $s_X$ with $$s_XL=s_0l,\qquad Ls_X=\lambda(X_s),$$ and with the $g_j$ reducing to their specified rational functions on $X_s$. This also explains why no extra ramification or defect is hidden in the passage to the good fiber.

*Cut down to a curve.* Over $\lambda$, choose a general flag of $d-1$ hyperplanes in the fixed projective embedding. Make the final section $C$ avoid the omitted codimension-two set and meet the $D_{i,s}$ transversely. The intersections with different $D_{i,s}$ are disjoint. The final section is smooth and integral. Apply Lemma <a href="#tests:bertini" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tests:bertini">14</a> successively to the finite list of covering components as well as to the reduced underlying base sections; each restricted covering component remains integral over $U_s\cap C$. At every step the varieties to which irreducibility is applied have dimension at least two, including the last surface-to-curve step. Smoothness of the restricted cover follows from its étaleness over the smooth curve open.

For completeness, intermediate scheme sections need only have irreducible support and be smooth at the generic points used by the flag. All those points lie in the original smooth locus. Choose the hyperplanes also to avoid the finitely many associated points at each stage, so their equations are non-zero-divisors. The hyperplane exact sequences give $$P_C(n)=\Delta^{d-1}P_X(n),\qquad
 \Delta P(n)=P(n)-P(n-1).$$ As $C$ is smooth integral, its genus is consequently fixed by the embedded model $X$; denote it by $G_X$. All conditions imposed on the flag are finitely many nonempty open conditions. They can be met over the infinite algebraically closed field $\lambda$, even when it is countable.

Compose $s_X$ with the successive divisorial valuations of this flag. For the first composition, the relative value group is the innermost convex copy of $\mathbb Z$, and its residue transcendence degree drops by one. Every proper coarsening kills this copy of $\mathbb Z$ and therefore has all its values supplied by constants. The first composition is thus a quasi-prime divisor. The remaining flag valuations are prime divisors over the residue constants, giving a quasi-prime $(d-1)$-divisor $w$. All $g_j$ are units at every generic point in the flag, so $w(g_j)=0$, and $Lw=\lambda(C)$.

*Check relations and orders.* An old relation $\prod_jg_j^{n_j}=b^q$ specializes: since $w(g_j)=0$, torsion-freeness of the value group gives $w(b)=0$. Thus the old relation kernel in $(\mathbb Z/q)^r$ is contained in the new one. On the other hand, every component of the root cover restricted to $U_s\cap C$ is integral and has degree $|H|$. Kummer theory gives the same order $|H|$ for the subgroup generated by the residue classes. The two finite relation kernels therefore have the same order, so they are equal.

Finally, $C$ meets $D_{i,s}$ in exactly $\deg D_i$ distinct transverse points. At each such point the order of $\overline g_j$ equals the unchanged integer $\operatorname{ord}_{D_i}(g_j)$. There are no other zeros or poles. This proves <a href="#tests:support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:support">[tests:support]</a>, in fact for every divisor $m$ of $q$, and completes the construction. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:pencil"></a>
### A uniform bound for pencils

The preceding construction tests a finite collection of functions at a finite Kummer level. We now combine such tests with five fixed members of a pencil. Riemann–Hurwitz bounds the degree of every residual pencil, which in turn bounds the support of the entire original pencil. Bounded-genus curve sections and Riemann–Hurwitz already underlie the finite-support argument of Bogomolov and Tschinkel (F. Bogomolov and Tschinkel 2011, sec. 6, proof of Proposition 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[BT2011](#ref-BT2011)]. Here the fixed anchors give one bound for every member of the pencil, and the finite residual tests allow arbitrary algebraically closed constants.

Keep the model $X$ of the preceding section and the Kummer isomorphism $\Theta:\widehat K\to\widehat L$. For $h\in\widehat L$ and $e\ge1$, define $$S_e(h)=\{D\subset X\text{ prime divisor}:
                \operatorname{ord}_D(h)\not\equiv0\pmod{\ell^e}\},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Supp}_X(h)=\{D:\operatorname{ord}_D(h)\ne0\}.$$ The order maps extend continuously to $\widehat L$. Each $S_e(h)$ is finite, because $h$ modulo $\ell^e$ is represented by an ordinary rational function. The exact support need not be finite a priori. The sets $S_e(h)$ increase with $e$, and their union is $\operatorname{Supp}_X(h)$.

<a id="stmt-pencil-uniform"></a>
<div id="pencil:uniform" class="theorem">

**Theorem 15** (Uniform pencil support). *For every $t\in K\setminus k$, there is a finite constant $B_t$ such that $$\sum_{D\in\operatorname{Supp}_X(\Theta[t-a])}\deg D\le B_t
 \qquad\text{for every }a\in k.$$ In particular, all these supports are finite, with a bound independent of the member of the pencil.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pencil-uniform"><summary>Proof of stmt-pencil-uniform</summary>

*Proof.* Choose five distinct anchors $a_1,\ldots,a_5\in k$, including $0$. In characteristic zero include $1$ and $\ell$ as well. Put $h_a=\Theta[t-a]$ and $$N_i=\sum_{D\in S_1(h_{a_i})}\deg D\quad(1\le i\le5),
 \qquad c_\ell=5(1-1/\ell)-2=3-5/\ell.$$ The $N_i$ are finite before any exact-support assertion is known. Moreover $c_\ell\ge1/2$, so the constant <a id="pencil:constant"></a>$$\label{pencil:constant}
 B=\max\left\{1,\frac{2G_X-2+\sum_{i=1}^5N_i}{c_\ell}\right\}$$ is finite and depends only on $X$, $\Theta$, $t$ and the fixed anchors. We prove the theorem with $B_t=2B$.

Fix one additional $a\in k$, and list the distinct elements among the five anchors and $a$ as $a_j$. Write $u_j=t-a_j$. For all sufficiently large $e$, the classes $[u_j]$ modulo $q=\ell^e$ are nonzero and pairwise distinct and generate a noncyclic subgroup. Indeed the functions $t-a_j$ are independent modulo constants: a multiplicative relation would give a constant rational function of the transcendental element $t$ and hence have all exponents zero. Their images in the free abelian group $K^\times/k^\times$ are therefore independent. Integral evaluations detect nonzero elements and nonzero two-by-two determinants, which remain nonzero modulo a sufficiently large power of $\ell$. Equivalently, one can prolong two distinct point valuations of $k(t)$ to $K$ to obtain a diagonal evaluation matrix with nonzero integer entries. The necessary lower bound on $e$ may depend on $a$.

Choose representatives $g_j\in L^\times$ of $h_{a_j}$ modulo $q$, and apply Lemma <a href="#tests:residual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tests:residual">13</a>. It gives $w$, a residual curve $C$, and the same noncyclic subgroup with distinct nonzero generators in $(Lw)^\times/(Lw)^{\times q}$. Evaluation on $D_w/I_w=W_{Lw}$ detects these classes. By Proposition <a href="#local:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:transport">10</a>, there is a corresponding quasi-prime $(d-1)$-divisor $v$ of $K/k$, and the evaluations of the $u_j$ on $D_v$ modulo $q$ are likewise nonzero, distinct and noncyclic.

*Normalize the residual pencil.* We first show that <a id="pencil:no-relative-value"></a>$$\label{pencil:no-relative-value}
 v(k(t))=vk.$$ Otherwise $v(k(t))/vk$ is a nonzero subgroup of the finitely generated free group $vK/vk$. The valuation transcendence-degree inequality for $k(t)/k$ makes it cyclic and gives $k(t)v=kv$. Every function of value in $vk$ then becomes a principal unit after multiplication by constants. Since $D_v$ kills constants and principal units, evaluation on $D_v$ factors through this cyclic relative value group. Its image modulo $q$ is cyclic, a contradiction.

<a id="pencil:residual-linear"></a>
All the $v(u_j)$ have a common value $\gamma\in vk$. For if $v(u_j)<v(u_h)$, then $u_j/(a_h-a_j)=1+u_h/(a_h-a_j)$ is a principal unit, forcing the evaluation of $u_j$ to vanish. Also $v(a_j-a_h)=\gamma$ whenever $j\ne h$: a strictly larger value would make $u_j/u_h$ a principal unit and force two evaluations to coincide. Choose $b\in k^\times$ with $v(b)=\gamma$, and fix one listed anchor $a_0$. Set $$\tau_0=\overline{(t-a_0)/b},\qquad
 \beta_j=\overline{(a_j-a_0)/b}.$$ These expressions are defined, the $\beta_j$ are distinct elements of $kv$, and $$\label{pencil:residual-linear}
 \overline{u_j/b}=\tau_0-\beta_j.$$ The element $\tau_0$ is nonconstant: a constant residue would make $(t-a_0)/b$ a constant times a principal unit and give zero evaluation.

The residue characteristic is different from $\ell$. This is automatic if $k$ has positive characteristic. In characteristic zero, the anchor differences $1-0$ and $\ell-0$ have the same value, so $v(\ell)=v(1)=0$. Thus $\ell$ is nonzero in $kv$ in that case as well.

<a id="pencil:genus"></a>
*Compare the two residual curves.* The constants $lw$ are algebraic over a finite field. Proposition <a href="#local:curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:curves">12</a> therefore gives the same assertion for $kv$ and identifies the full families of point inertia lines in $W_{Lw}$ and $W_{Kv}$. Let $C'/kv$ be the smooth projective curve with function field $Kv$. By Lemma <a href="#prelim:curve-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:curve-kernel">5</a>, the mod-$\ell$ kernels of the point order maps have dimensions $2g(C)$ and $2g(C')$. The residual isomorphism identifies these kernels, and hence $$\label{pencil:genus}
 g(C')=g(C)\le G_X.$$ Generators of matched point inertia lines differ by units of $\mathbb Z_\ell$. Consequently the number of nonzero point orders modulo $\ell$ or $q$ is preserved by the comparison. Because constants have zero Kummer class, evaluation identifies the classes of the functions in <a href="#pencil:residual-linear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pencil:residual-linear">[pencil:residual-linear]</a> with those of $\overline g_j$ modulo $q$.

*Use the five anchors to bound the degree.* Write $p=\operatorname{char}(kv)>0$, using the finite-constant recognition above. Remove inseparability by writing $\tau_0=\tau^{p^r}$ with $r\ge0$ maximal. Such an $r$ is finite, as a nonzero integer point order of $\tau_0$ cannot be divisible by arbitrarily high powers of $p$. The function $\tau$ gives a separable morphism $\tau:C'\to\mathbb P^1$ of some degree $n\ge1$. Let $\beta'_j$ be the $p^r$-th root of $\beta_j$ in $kv$. These constants are distinct, and $$\tau_0-\beta_j=(\tau-\beta'_j)^{p^r}.$$ Since $p\ne\ell$, this power does not affect whether any point order is zero modulo $\ell$ or $q$.

Over the value $\beta'_i$ corresponding to anchor $i$, at most $N_i$ points have ramification index not divisible by $\ell$. Indeed such a point contributes a nonzero order modulo $\ell$ to $\tau_0-\beta_i$; transport to $C$ and <a href="#tests:support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:support">[tests:support]</a> give the bound $N_i$. Every other point of that fiber has ramification index at least $\ell$, so there are at most $n/\ell$ of them. If $r_i$ denotes the total number of points in the fiber, then $$r_i\le N_i+n/\ell,\qquad
 \sum_{P\mid\beta'_i}(e_P-1)=n-r_i
       \ge(1-1/\ell)n-N_i.$$ For a separable morphism of smooth curves, the different exponent at $P$ is at least $e_P-1$, including in the presence of wild ramification. The five fibers are disjoint. Riemann–Hurwitz therefore yields $$2g(C')-2+2n
 \ge\sum_{i=1}^5\sum_{P\mid\beta'_i}(e_P-1)
 \ge5(1-1/\ell)n-\sum_{i=1}^5N_i.$$ Together with <a href="#pencil:genus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pencil:genus">[pencil:genus]</a> this gives $c_\ell n\le
2G_X-2+\sum_iN_i$, and hence $n\le B$. This argument uses the different inequality, not the tame Riemann–Hurwitz formula; see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, sec. 53.12, Tag 0C1B) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].

<a id="pencil:finite-level-bound"></a>
*Pass from finite tests to exact supports.* For every tested $j$, all zeros and poles of $\tau-\beta'_j$ lie in two fibers, over $\beta'_j$ and infinity. There are at most $2n$ such points. The number of its nonzero point orders modulo $q$ is therefore at most $2B$. Transport this bound to $C$ and apply <a href="#tests:support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tests:support">[tests:support]</a> to obtain $$\label{pencil:finite-level-bound}
 \sum_{D\in S_e(h_a)}\deg D\le2B.$$ The right side is independent of $a$, $e$, the finite descent data and the residual test. For each fixed $a$, the inequality holds for every sufficiently large $e$.

Finally $S_e(h_a)$ increases to $\operatorname{Supp}_X(h_a)$. If the exact support had total degree greater than $2B$, a finite subset would already have degree greater than $2B$; that subset would lie in $S_e(h_a)$ for all sufficiently large $e$, contradicting <a href="#pencil:finite-level-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pencil:finite-level-bound">[pencil:finite-level-bound]</a>. Every prime divisor has positive integer degree, so the exact support is finite. This proves the asserted uniform bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pencil-finite"></a>
<div id="pencil:finite" class="corollary">

**Corollary 16**. *For every $z\in K^\times$, the class $\Theta[z]$ has finite support on $X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pencil-finite"><summary>Proof of stmt-pencil-finite</summary>

*Proof.* If $z\notin k$, apply Theorem <a href="#pencil:uniform" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:uniform">15</a> to $t=z$ and $a=0$. If $z\in k^\times$, its Kummer class is zero because $k^\times$ is divisible. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:curve-recovery"></a>
### Recovering the curve subfields

A *curve subfield* of $K/k$ will mean a subfield $E\subset K$ containing $k$, relatively algebraically closed in $K$, and of transcendence degree one over $k$. Such a field is finite over $k(t)$ for each $t\in E\setminus k$. We identify its completed multiplicative group $\widehat E$ with its image in $\widehat K$ by Lemma <a href="#prelim:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:completion">3</a>.

<a id="stmt-recovery-curves"></a>
<div id="recovery:curves" class="theorem">

**Theorem 17**. *The map $\Theta$ gives a bijection between the completed curve subfields of $K/k$ and those of $L/l$. More precisely, for every curve subfield $E\subset K$ there is a unique curve subfield $P\subset L$ such that $$\Theta(\widehat E)=\widehat P.$$*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#pencil:uniform" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:uniform">15</a> bounds the degrees of the prime divisors detected by a pencil. We first show that infinitely many occur. Their incidence family gives containment in a completed curve field over a finite extension of $L$. Intersections and norms then descend this containment to $L$, and the inverse correspondence gives equality.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The divisors detected by a pencil

Fix $t\in K\setminus k$, let $E$ be the relative algebraic closure of $k(t)$ in $K$, and put $$h_a=\Theta[t-a],\qquad
 \mathcal S=\bigcup_{a\in k}\operatorname{Supp}_X(h_a).$$ Here $X/l$ is the fixed normal projective model used in Theorem <a href="#pencil:uniform" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:uniform">15</a>. That theorem bounds the degree of every member of $\mathcal S$ by a constant depending on $t$ and $X$.

<a id="stmt-recovery-residue-powers"></a>
<div id="recovery:residue-powers" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:recovery-residue-powers"></a>
**Lemma 18**. *The set $\mathcal S$ is infinite. For every $z\in E^\times$, every $j\geq1$, and every representative $g\in L^\times$ of $\Theta[z]\bmod\ell^j$, all but finitely many $D\in\mathcal S$ satisfy $$\label{eq:recovery-residue-powers}
 \operatorname{ord}_D(g)=0,
 \qquad g|_D\in l(D)^{\times\ell^j}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-recovery-residue-powers"><summary>Proof of stmt-recovery-residue-powers</summary>

*Proof.* The classes $[t-a]$ are independent in $k(t)^\times/\ell$, as evaluation at their distinct zeros shows. The kernel of $k(t)^\times/\ell\to K^\times/\ell$ is finite dimensional: adjoining roots for any finite independent subset of that kernel gives an elementary abelian $\ell$-extension contained in the finite extension $E/k(t)$. Thus their images span an infinite-dimensional subspace of $K^\times/\ell$, and the same is true of the $h_a$ in $\widehat L/\ell=L^\times/\ell$.

The kernel of divisor evaluation $$L^\times/\ell\longrightarrow
 \bigoplus_{D\in X^{(1)}}\mathbb F_\ell$$ is finite dimensional. Indeed, take a smooth dense open $U\subset X$. A function whose divisor on $U$ is divisible by $\ell$ defines a $\mu_\ell$-torsor on $U$: its divisor divided by $\ell$ is Cartier. The kernel therefore embeds in $H^1_{\mathrm{\acute et}}(U,\mu_\ell)$, which is finite dimensional by Deligne’s finiteness theorem (Deligne 1977, Théorèmes de finitude, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[DeligneSGA4half](#ref-DeligneSGA4half)]. If $\mathcal S$ were finite, both the image and the kernel of divisor evaluation on the pencil span would be finite dimensional, a contradiction.

For $D\in\mathcal S$, let $w_D$ be its divisorial valuation and let $v$ be the quasi-prime divisor of $K/k$ supplied by Proposition <a href="#local:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:transport">10</a>. Its inertia detects some $[t-a]$. Consequently $vE/vk$ is nonzero inside the rank-one group $vK/vk$. The valuation transcendence-degree inequality applied to $E/k$ gives $\operatorname{trdeg}(Ev/kv)=0$, so $Ev=kv$ because $kv$ is algebraically closed.

If $\operatorname{ord}_D(\Theta[z])=0$, inertia evaluation implies $v(z)\in vk$. Multiplying $z$ first by a constant to give it value zero, and then by a constant with the reciprocal residue, makes it a principal unit. Thus $D_v$ kills $[z]$, and $D_{w_D}$ kills $\Theta[z]$. By Corollary <a href="#pencil:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pencil:finite">16</a>, the excluded $D$ form a finite set. Discard also the finitely many prime divisors in the ordinary divisor of $g$. For every remaining $D$, $g$ is a unit at $D$, and its residue has zero evaluation modulo $\ell^j$ against $D_{w_D}/I_{w_D}=W_{l(D)}$. Finite-level Kummer duality gives <a href="#eq:recovery-residue-powers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recovery-residue-powers">[eq:recovery-residue-powers]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### One incidence curve for every Kummer test

The following geometric lemma permits an arbitrary collection of tests. In particular, neither the constants nor the collection must be countable or uncountable.

<a id="stmt-recovery-incidence"></a>
<div id="recovery:incidence" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19**. *Let $l$ be algebraically closed, let $X/l$ be a normal integral projective variety of dimension $d\geq2$, and let $\mathcal S$ be an infinite set of prime divisors of bounded degree in a fixed projective embedding. Let $\mathcal A$ be any collection of pairs $(g,q)$, where $g\in l(X)^\times$ and $q\geq1$ is prime to $\operatorname{char}l$. Suppose that, for each pair separately, all but finitely many $D\in\mathcal S$ satisfy $$\operatorname{ord}_D(g)=0,
 \qquad g|_D\in l(D)^{\times q}.$$ There exist a finite field extension $M/l(X)$ and a curve field $P_0/l$ contained in $M$ such that $M/P_0$ is regular and $$g\in(M\overline{P_0})^{\times q}
 \quad\text{for every }(g,q)\in\mathcal A.$$ The same fields $M$ and $P_0$ work for the entire collection.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-recovery-incidence"><summary>Proof of stmt-recovery-incidence</summary>

*Proof.* *Place the divisors in a bounded family.* Integral subvarieties of fixed dimension and bounded degree in $\mathbb P^N_l$ have only finitely many Hilbert polynomials (Grothendieck 1961, sec. 2 and Lemma 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[GrothHilbert1961](#ref-GrothHilbert1961)]; see also (Tanaka 2023, Proposition 5.3) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Tanaka2023](#ref-Tanaka2023)].

The Hilbert points of $\mathcal S$ therefore lie in a finite union of projective Hilbert schemes of $X$, with their universal flat families (Grothendieck 1961, Theorem 3.2 and the Hilbert specialization after Proposition 3.8) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[GrothHilbert1961](#ref-GrothHilbert1961)]. Their closure has a positive-dimensional irreducible component on which they are dense. Give it its reduced structure and shrink to an integral variety $T$ such that the universal family $$Z\subset T\times X\longrightarrow T$$ has geometrically integral divisor fibers. This is possible by constructibility of geometric integrality (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 0579, 055B) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]; the family is flat by construction.

*Fix one incidence curve.* Choose an integral locally closed curve $T'\subset T$ now, before making any of the tests in $\mathcal A$, and put $Z'=Z\times_T T'$. Both $Z$ and $Z'$ are integral. Indeed, flatness over an integral base excludes vertical components and embeds affine coordinate rings in their generic-fiber localizations, while the generic fibers are geometrically integral. Both incidence maps to $X$ are dominant. Otherwise a proper closed subset of $X$ would contain infinitely many distinct prime-divisor fibers; each would have to be one of its finitely many components of dimension $d-1$. Distinct parameter points give distinct divisors because they are distinct Hilbert points.

Set $$M=l(Z'),\qquad P_0=l(T').$$ Dominance embeds $l(X)$ into $M$. Since $\dim Z'=d$, this is a finite extension. Geometric integrality of the generic fiber of $Z'\to T'$ means that $M/P_0$ is regular.

Figure <a href="#fig:incidence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:incidence">1</a> records the two projections that determine these fields.

<figure id="fig:incidence">

<figcaption>The left square is Cartesian. The composite $Z′ → X$ is dominant and generically finite, giving $l(X) ⊂ M = l(Z′)$; the vertical map gives $P_{0} = l(T′) ⊂ M$. The curve $T′$ is fixed before the tests $(g,q)$. Auxiliary normal covers used to split a test may depend on that test, but $M$ and $P_{0}$ do not.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-incidence"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:incidence`)

Visual witness description of PDF page 20 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A commutative diagram with five nodes arranged in two rows: top row contains $Z'$, $Z$, and $X$ (from left to right); bottom row contains $T'$ and $T$ (from left to right, aligned under $Z'$ and $Z$). Directed arrows: a horizontal arrow from $Z'$ to $Z$; a horizontal arrow from $Z$ to $X$ labeled $\mathrm{pr}_X$ above the arrow; a vertical downward arrow from $Z'$ to $T'$; a vertical downward arrow from $Z$ to $T$; and a horizontal arrow from $T'$ to $T$.
```


*Extend each splitting to the fixed curve.* It remains to prove all the power assertions without changing $T'$. Fix one pair $(g,q)$. Let $U\subset X$ be the unit locus of $g$, and let $$Y=Z^{\mathrm{sm}/T}\cap\operatorname{pr}_X^{-1}(U).$$ Then $Y\to T$ is smooth. Its fibers over the generic points of $T$ and of $T'$ are nonempty and geometrically integral: geometric integrality supplies a dense smooth locus, and dominance of the two incidence maps supplies the unit condition. Over $Y$ consider the degree-$q$ finite étale cover $$C_g=\operatorname{Spec}_Y\mathcal O_Y[u]/(u^q-g).$$ On every tested fiber satisfying the hypothesis and meeting $Y$, a rational $q$-th root extends to a regular unit by normality of the smooth fiber. Thus the cover splits into $q$ copies on those fibers. The number of geometric irreducible components is constant on a dense open of $T$ (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 055A) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. That number must be $q$, by the dense set of tested fibers. A degree-$q$ finite étale cover of a normal integral scheme with $q$ components has degree one on each component. It follows that the cover is split on the geometric generic fiber over $T$.

This last dense open need not meet $T'$. To pass to the fixed curve, descend the generic splitting to a finite extension of $l(T)$ and normalize $T$ in it, obtaining a finite surjective morphism $\widetilde T\to T$. The scheme $\widetilde Y=Y\times_T\widetilde T$ is normal because it is smooth over a normal base (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 034F) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. It is integral because its generic fiber is geometrically integral and it is flat over the integral base. The pulled-back cover is generically split and finite étale over this normal integral scheme, hence split everywhere: each component is finite birational over the normal target and is an isomorphism (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Lemma 58.11.2, Tag 0BQL) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. Surjectivity of $\widetilde T\to T$ provides a lift of a geometric point above the generic point of $T'$. Restricting the split cover to that point proves that $g$ has a $q$-th root in $M\overline{P_0}$. The auxiliary finite extension may depend on $(g,q)$; the already chosen fields $M$ and $P_0$ do not. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Kummer descent and intersections

We next turn geometric splitting into membership in a completed curve field. Two elementary facts keep track of the finite extension introduced by the incidence construction.

<a id="stmt-recovery-kummer-descent"></a>
<div id="recovery:kummer-descent" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20**. *Let $M/P$ be a regular field extension, let $q$ be prime to the characteristic, and suppose $\mu_q\subset P$. If $g\in M^\times$ becomes a $q$-th power in $M\overline P$, then its class in $M^\times/M^{\times q}$ comes from a unique class in $P^\times/P^{\times q}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-recovery-kummer-descent"><summary>Proof of stmt-recovery-kummer-descent</summary>

*Proof.* A $q$-th root $r$ of $g$ is separable over $M$, so it already belongs to $MP^{\mathrm{sep}}$: the remaining constant extension is purely inseparable. Choose a finite Galois extension $P'/P$ with $r\in MP'$. Regularity identifies $\operatorname{Gal}(MP'/M)$ with $\operatorname{Gal}(P'/P)$. The ratios $\sigma(r)/r\in\mu_q$ form a character of this group. By Hilbert’s Theorem 90, there is $b\in P'^\times$ with these same ratios. Then $r/b\in M$ and $b^q\in P$, giving $g=b^q(r/b)^q$. Finally, if an element of $P$ has a $q$-th root in $M$, that root is algebraic over $P$ and therefore belongs to $P$. This proves uniqueness. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-recovery-intersection"></a>
<div id="recovery:intersection" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *Let $N/l$ be a function field over algebraically closed constants of characteristic different from $\ell$. If $P$ and $Q$ are distinct curve subfields of $N/l$, then $\widehat P\cap\widehat Q\subset\widehat N$ has finite $\mathbb Z_\ell$-rank.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-recovery-intersection"><summary>Proof of stmt-recovery-intersection</summary>

*Proof.* The compositum $PQ$ has transcendence degree two over $l$; otherwise relative algebraic closedness in $N$ would force $P=Q$. Let $C_P$ and $C_Q$ be the smooth projective curves of these fields. The product is integral, and its function field identifies with $PQ$: the rational map defined by the two subfields has two-dimensional, hence dense, image in $C_P\times C_Q$.

For a point $x\in C_P(l)$, take the valuation of the divisor $\{x\}\times C_Q$. Extend it by a Gauss valuation over a transcendence basis for $N/PQ$, then prolong it across the remaining finite extension. Its normalized order $w$ on $N$ is zero on $Q^\times$ and restricts to $e\operatorname{ord}_x$ on $P^\times$, for some integer $e>0$. This construction allows inseparable extensions and all ramification indices.

An element of $\widehat P\cap\widehat Q$ has $w$-order zero by its $Q$-representation. Its $x$-order is therefore zero, since multiplication by $e$ is injective on $\mathbb Z_\ell$. This holds for every $x$. By Lemma <a href="#prelim:curve-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:curve-kernel">5</a>, the intersection embeds in the finite-rank Tate module of $\operatorname{Pic}^0(C_P)$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-recovery-intersection-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-recovery-intersection</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#recovery:curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:curves">17</a>.* Let $E\subset K$ be a curve subfield, and choose $t\in E\setminus k$. Relative algebraic closedness makes $E$ the relative algebraic closure of $k(t)$ in $K$. Apply Lemmas <a href="#recovery:residue-powers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:residue-powers">18</a> and <a href="#recovery:incidence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:incidence">19</a> to one representative of each $\Theta[z]\bmod\ell^j$, for $z\in E^\times$ and $j\geq1$. We obtain fields $L\subset M$ and $P_0\subset M$ as in the incidence lemma.

Put $H=\Theta(\widehat E)$. We claim that its image in $\widehat M$ lies in $\widehat P_0$. At level $\ell^j$, every element of $\widehat E$ is represented by an actual $z\in E^\times$. Lemma <a href="#recovery:kummer-descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:kummer-descent">20</a> puts its image in $P_0^\times/P_0^{\times\ell^j}$. These classes are unique because $P_0$ is relatively algebraically closed in $M$, and their uniqueness makes them compatible as $j$ varies. Taking inverse limits proves the claim. Completion maps across $M/L$ are injective by Lemma <a href="#prelim:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:completion">3</a>.

Choose a finite normal extension $N/L$ containing $M$, allowing an inseparable part, and let $P_N$ be the relative algebraic closure of $P_0$ in $N$. It is a curve subfield of $N/l$. The image of $H$ lies in $\widehat P_N$. Every $\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}_L(N)$ fixes $H$, so $H$ also lies in $\widehat{\sigma(P_N)}$. It has infinite rank: $\widehat E/\ell$ has infinite dimension, as the pencil argument in Lemma <a href="#recovery:residue-powers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:residue-powers">18</a> shows. Indeed, lifts of any finite mod-$\ell$ independent set are $\mathbb Z_\ell$-independent: a nonzero relation, divided by the smallest $\ell$-power in its coefficients, would reduce to a nonzero mod-$\ell$ relation. Torsion freeness justifies this division. Lemma <a href="#recovery:intersection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:intersection">21</a> therefore forces $\sigma(P_N)=P_N$ for every $\sigma$.

Set $P_L=P_N\cap L$. This field has transcendence degree one over $l$. Indeed, for $G=\operatorname{Aut}_L(N)$, the field $P_N^G$ has transcendence degree one and lies in $N^G$. In characteristic zero $N^G=L$. In characteristic $p>0$, $N^G/L$ is finite purely inseparable, so a common $p^a$-th power sends $P_N^G$ into $P_L$. Moreover $P_L$ is relatively algebraically closed in $L$: an element of $L$ algebraic over $P_L$ is algebraic over $P_N$, hence lies in $P_N$. Taking the relative algebraic closure of any nonconstant rational subfield shows that $P_L/l$ is finitely generated.

The norm $N_{N/L}$ sends $P_N^\times$ into $P_L^\times$. In fact, if $s$ is the inseparable degree of $N/L$, then $$N_{N/L}(x)=\left(\prod_{\sigma\in G}\sigma(x)\right)^s;$$ the right side lies in $P_N$ because $P_N$ is $G$-stable, and the norm lies in $L$. On completions, norm composed with inclusion is multiplication by $[N:L]$. We conclude that $[N:L]H\subset\widehat P_L$. The latter is saturated in $\widehat L$ by Lemma <a href="#prelim:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:completion">3</a>, so $H\subset\widehat P_L$. Here the prime-to-$\ell$ factor of $[N:L]$ is a unit of $\mathbb Z_\ell$, and saturation removes its remaining $\ell$-power factor.

Apply the same argument to $\Theta^{-1}$. It gives a curve subfield $E'\subset K$ with $$\widehat E\subset\Theta^{-1}(\widehat P_L)
 \subset\widehat E'.$$ The infinite-rank intersection forces $E'=E$ by Lemma <a href="#recovery:intersection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:intersection">21</a>. Both inclusions are therefore equalities. That lemma also proves uniqueness. Reversing $K$ and $L$ proves the asserted bijection. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Divisorial valuations and rational quotients

We can now distinguish valuations trivial on the constants. Throughout this subsection, a *divisorial valuation* means such a quasi-prime divisor, with its discrete value group normalized to $\mathbb Z$.

The following criterion adapts Topaz’s Lemma A.7 (Topaz 2025, Lemma A.7) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[TopazTorelli2025](#ref-TopazTorelli2025)] to completed multiplicative groups. The proof below verifies the needed hypotheses in this setting.

<a id="stmt-recovery-divisorial"></a>
<div id="recovery:divisorial" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:recovery-divisorial"></a>
**Proposition 22**. *A quasi-prime divisor $v$ of $K/k$ is divisorial if and only if $$\label{eq:recovery-divisorial}
 D_v^\perp\cap\widehat E=0$$ for some curve subfield $E$ of $K/k$. Consequently $\varphi$ preserves divisorial inertia and decomposition groups in both directions.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-recovery-divisorial"><summary>Proof of stmt-recovery-divisorial</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose $v$ is trivial on $k$. Its residue field has transcendence degree $d-1\geq1$. Choose $t$ with transcendental residue, and let $E$ be the relative algebraic closure of $k(t)$ in $K$. The valuation is trivial on $k(t)$ and hence on its algebraic extension $E$. Thus $E$ embeds into $Kv$. Its relative algebraic closure in $Kv$ is finite over $E$, so the induced completion map is injective: factor it through that finite extension and then a relatively algebraically closed inclusion. Residual evaluation by $D_v/I_v=W_{Kv}$ proves <a href="#eq:recovery-divisorial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recovery-divisorial">[eq:recovery-divisorial]</a>.

Conversely, suppose $v|k$ is nontrivial, and let $E$ be any curve subfield. Choose $x\in E\setminus k$, replacing it by $x^{-1}$ if necessary so that $v(x)\geq0$, and choose $c\in k$ with $v(c)>0$. Then $1+cx$ is a nonconstant principal unit. Its class in $\widehat E$ is nonzero and is killed by $D_v$, so <a href="#eq:recovery-divisorial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recovery-divisorial">[eq:recovery-divisorial]</a> fails. The criterion is invariant under $\Theta$ by Theorem <a href="#recovery:curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:curves">17</a> and the local correspondence. ◻

</details>

For a curve subfield $E\subset K$, restriction gives a surjection $$\rho_E:W_K\longrightarrow W_E.$$ This is the continuous surjection of Lemma <a href="#prelim:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:completion">3</a>.

<a id="stmt-recovery-rational"></a>
<div id="recovery:rational" class="proposition">

**Proposition 23**. *The point inertia lines of every curve subfield $E$ are precisely the saturations of the nonzero images $\rho_E(I_v)$ for divisorial valuations $v$ of $K/k$. The correspondence of Theorem <a href="#recovery:curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:curves">17</a> preserves these lines and the genus. In particular, it matches exactly the relatively algebraically closed rational subfields.*

<a id="eq:recovery-quotient-diagram"></a>
*If $\Theta(\widehat E)=\widehat P$, the induced continuous isomorphism $\varphi_E:W_P\to W_E$ makes the diagram $$\label{eq:recovery-quotient-diagram}
\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large]
 W_L \arrow[r,"\varphi"] \arrow[d,two heads,"\rho_P"']
   & W_K \arrow[d,two heads,"\rho_E"] \\
 W_P \arrow[r,"\varphi_E"'] & W_E
\end{tikzcd}$$ commute and matches the point inertia lines on its targets. For rational $E$ and $P$, these are the geometric rational quotient diagrams, with their point decomposition data.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-recovery-rational"><summary>Proof of stmt-recovery-rational</summary>

*Proof.* A divisorial valuation restricted nontrivially to $E$ is an integer multiple of a point valuation on its smooth projective curve: its value group is a nonzero subgroup of $\mathbb Z$, and it is trivial on $k$. Conversely, extend any point valuation of $E$ by a Gauss valuation over a transcendence basis for $K/E$, then prolong it over the remaining finite extension. The resulting valuation is divisorial on $K/k$. Its restriction is $e\operatorname{ord}_x$ for some $e>0$. Saturation of the nonzero image recovers $\mathbb Z_\ell\operatorname{ord}_x$, including when $\ell$ divides $e$.

The dual of $\Theta|_{\widehat E}$ defines $\varphi_E$. Evaluation against elements of $\widehat E$ proves the commutativity of <a href="#eq:recovery-quotient-diagram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recovery-quotient-diagram">[eq:recovery-quotient-diagram]</a>. Proposition <a href="#recovery:divisorial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:divisorial">22</a> and the preceding description of point lines show that $\varphi_E$ matches them. Their generators may differ by units, which do not change the common kernel of point-order evaluations modulo $\ell$. Its dimension is twice the genus by Lemma <a href="#prelim:curve-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:curve-kernel">5</a>. Hence genus is preserved. A smooth projective curve over an algebraically closed field has genus zero exactly when its function field is rational.

If $k(t)$ is relatively algebraically closed in $K$, the extension $K/k(t)$ is regular. Only separability needs explanation. In characteristic $p>0$, relative algebraic closedness implies $t\notin K^p$. Since $k$ is perfect, $t$ can be included in a separating transcendence basis for $K/k$, proving that $K/k(t)$ is separably generated. Characteristic zero is immediate. Thus these are exactly the rational subfields defined by general elements. Finally, the residue field at a point of a rational curve is the algebraically closed constant field, whose character group is zero. Point decomposition therefore equals point inertia, and the diagram also carries the required point decomposition data. ◻

</details>

The curve correspondence has consequently recovered both the true divisorial valuations and all geometric rational quotients from the original datum. The final reconstruction theorem can now be applied with these intrinsically determined objects.

<a id="sec:reconstruction"></a>
### From the recovered quotients to the field

We have recovered the geometric data needed for the last step. We now specify that data and Pop’s reconstruction theorems, so that their application uses exactly the original group input.

For a function field $F/\kappa$, a *divisorial flag* is a sequence of valuations obtained by taking a prime divisor trivial on $\kappa$, then a prime divisor on its residue field, and continuing in this way. The empty flag is included. The *total decomposition graph* records, at each such flag, the abelian pro-$\ell$ group of the residue field, and at each next divisorial valuation its inertia and decomposition subgroups and the quotient map to the next residue group. Thus the graph retains the group maps as well as the flags. At a one-variable field over algebraically closed constants, its terminal data are precisely the point inertia lines: the residual groups at closed points vanish, so point decomposition equals point inertia.

We use the following two results of Pop.

<a id="stmt-reconstruction-pop"></a>
<div id="reconstruction:pop" class="proposition">

**Proposition 24** (Pop’s reconstruction theorems). *Let $F/\kappa$ and $F'/\kappa'$ be function fields of transcendence degree greater than one over algebraically closed fields of characteristic different from $\ell$.*

1.  *From $\Pi_F^c\to\Pi_F^a$ and the union of its true divisorial inertia groups, one recovers the total decomposition graph, functorially under class-two isomorphisms whose abelianizations preserve this union.*

2.  *Suppose an isomorphism between the total decomposition graphs of $F$ and $F'$ is compatible with all their geometric rational quotient diagrams. Then its map on the top abelian groups is induced, up to one unit in $\mathbb Z_\ell$, by an isomorphism of perfect closures in the opposite direction carrying the constant fields onto each other. That field isomorphism is unique modulo Frobenius twists in positive characteristic, and unique in characteristic zero.*

*In (2), a geometric rational quotient is restriction to $\kappa(u)\subset F$ with $F/\kappa(u)$ regular, together with the point inertia and decomposition data on its target. Compatibility means that restriction commutes with the graph isomorphisms and the induced isomorphisms of these targets.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-reconstruction-pop"><summary>Proof of stmt-reconstruction-pop</summary>

*Reference and hypotheses.* Part (1) is (Pop 2018, Proposition 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Pop2018](#ref-Pop2018)]; the surrounding section assumes transcendence degree greater than one. Part (2) is the Isom statement of (Pop 2012a, Theorem 2.1(2)) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[PopBI2012](#ref-PopBI2012)], restating (Pop 2012b, Main Theorem) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[PopGraphs2012](#ref-PopGraphs2012)]. The stated theorem requires Bertini-type families of rational quotients; all geometric rational quotients form such a family. More explicitly, for algebraically independent $x,t$ with $x$ separating, the general functions $$ax+t,\qquad \frac{t}{a'x+a},\qquad
 \frac{a''t+a'x+a+1}{t+a'x+a}$$ in (Pop 2012b, Fact/Definition 43) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[PopGraphs2012](#ref-PopGraphs2012)] define relatively algebraically closed rational subfields for the prescribed cofinite choices of constants. Every one of these subfields is among the quotients in (2). Thus its hypotheses imply precisely the required Bertini compatibility. No restriction to finite-field closures or to dimension greater than two occurs in this Isom statement. ◻

</details>

We emphasize the distinction between the two citations. The later (Pop 2018, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Pop2018](#ref-Pop2018)] reconstructs rational quotients from divisorial inertia under a dimension-greater-than-two hypothesis. Here those quotients have already been recovered by Section <a href="#sec:curve-recovery" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:curve-recovery">6</a>, so that theorem is unnecessary.

<details id="proof-stmt-reconstruction-pop-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-reconstruction-pop</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* A field isomorphism $\alpha:K^i\to L^i$ induces a contravariant isomorphism of absolute Galois groups, well defined up to an inner automorphism. Purely inseparable extension does not change these groups. Abelianization removes the inner ambiguity, and the induced class-two map preserves the commutator. Thus it gives a member of $\mathop{\mathrm{Isom}}^c(\Pi_L^a,\Pi_K^a)$. In positive characteristic, absolute Frobenius on algebraic closures commutes with field automorphisms; composition with any integer Frobenius power therefore gives the same allowed orbit. This proves well-definedness of $\Phi_{K,L}$.

Conversely, let $\varphi:\Pi_L^a\to\Pi_K^a$ be bracket-compatible. Lemma <a href="#prelim:central-lift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prelim:central-lift">4</a> lifts it to a class-two isomorphism. After choosing Tate identifications, let $\Theta$ be its dual <a href="#eq:theta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta">[eq:theta]</a>. Proposition <a href="#local:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="local:transport">10</a> recovers the common relative transcendence degree and the quasi-divisorial pairs. The finite residual tests and the uniform support bound prove the completed curve-subfield correspondence in Theorem <a href="#recovery:curves" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:curves">17</a>. The recognition statements in Section <a href="#sec:curve-recovery" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:curve-recovery">6</a> then show that $\varphi$ preserves true divisorial inertia and every relatively algebraically closed rational quotient, including its point lines.

Proposition <a href="#recovery:rational" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="recovery:rational">23</a> gives the actual commuting quotient diagrams in <a href="#eq:recovery-quotient-diagram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recovery-quotient-diagram">[eq:recovery-quotient-diagram]</a>. Their vertical maps are surjective, and their target isomorphisms $\varphi_E$ match all point inertia lines. For rational targets these are the entire point decomposition data. Thus the same fixed $\varphi$ is compatible with every geometric rational quotient; no independent rescaling of the targets is needed.

Part (1) of Proposition <a href="#reconstruction:pop" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="reconstruction:pop">24</a> gives the total decomposition-graph isomorphism. The quotient squares also commute on these graphs. Indeed, restriction along an actual regular inclusion $E=k(t)\subset K$ is a geometric rational quotient morphism (Pop 2012a, sec. 2, Embeddings and Restrictions) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[PopBI2012](#ref-PopBI2012)]. At a flag vertex its map is obtained by restricting to the decomposition subgroup and passing to the inertia quotient (Pop 2012b, Definition/Remark 24 and Definition 25) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[PopGraphs2012](#ref-PopGraphs2012)]. If the flag restricts trivially to $E$, its inertia is killed, and the top square descends to the residual square. Otherwise its nonzero inertia image lies in a unique point line of $E$, where decomposition equals inertia and the residue group is trivial. The same description applies to $P\subset L$. Local images can have finite index: saturation identifies the point line, but the actual subgroup images and maps are retained. Applying these restriction and quotient operations successively along each flag therefore proves compatibility for the same fixed $\varphi$ and $\varphi_E$.

Part (2), applied to the recovered rational quotients, gives an isomorphism $\alpha:K^i\to L^i$ with $\alpha(k)=l$ and $[\varphi]=[\alpha^*]$. Choices of Tate identifications are absorbed by the one allowed global unit. This proves surjectivity. It also proves that different characteristics cannot give a bracket-compatible isomorphism.

Finally, two field isomorphisms with the same Galois-side orbit give the same compatible graph and quotient data after one global unit adjustment. The uniqueness assertion in Proposition <a href="#reconstruction:pop" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="reconstruction:pop">24</a> identifies them modulo Frobenius in positive characteristic, and identifies them outright in characteristic zero. On perfect closures the Frobenius powers are exactly the integer powers appearing in the definition of $\mathop{\mathrm{Isom}}_F^i(K,L)$. This proves injectivity and completes the theorem. ◻

</details>
